What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Yeah, um… I guess the short version is that I’m here because I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts of my life organized anymore. It’s gotten to the point where I’m sort of constantly juggling things—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and instead of being able to step back and decide what actually matters most, I end up just reacting to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment.\n\nAnd I know everyone gets busy, but this feels different. It’s like the pieces are all there, but I can’t get them into a usable order. I’ll start one thing because it’s making the most noise, then realize something more important was due, or that I’ve neglected a decision someone was waiting on. That’s really what finally pushed me to come in. I’m spending a lot of energy, but it doesn’t feel like I’m steering it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing on a single task for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, and it’s not always that I’m bored with the task, which is what’s frustrating. Like, I can be in the middle of coding something I actually care about, and I’ll have the whole structure in my head—what I’m trying to change, which file I’m in, why I opened it—and then Slack pings, or some little notification flashes in a browser tab, or even just a visual idea pops into my head, like “oh, that layout could work better if…” and suddenly I’ve lost the original thread.\n\nAnd then I’m not just distracted for thirty seconds. I’ll follow that thing. I’ll click over, check the message, maybe open another tab, look at some unrelated design reference, tweak something that wasn’t the point, and twenty minutes later I’m sitting there thinking, “Wait, what was I actually supposed to be doing?” It feels like my attention gets pulled sideways really easily, especially if the interruption has any novelty or visual hook to it. Then getting back into the original task takes more effort than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying when things are going well, exactly. I mean, I can second-guess whether I’m missing something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as worry in the way you mean it.\n\nLike if something is actually going smoothly, I don’t sit there feeling afraid something bad is definitely going to happen. It’s more that I might have this background sense of, “Okay, what am I forgetting?” But honestly I’m not sure whether that’s worry or just… trying to keep track of things. So I don’t know that I’ve noticed that as a major thing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious more often than not, even when there's no obvious reason to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least, not in the sense of feeling anxious most of the time for no clear reason.\n\nI can get tense when there’s something specific I’m trying to keep track of, or if I have that feeling that I may have forgotten something, but that feels tied to actual stuff going on rather than anxiety just showing up out of nowhere. If I’m sitting quietly and nothing obvious is pressing, I’m not usually in a constant anxious state.\n\nI guess I’d say I can be uneasy when I’m trying to mentally hold too many things at once, but I wouldn’t describe myself as anxious more often than not.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can definitely switch to something quickly if it catches my attention, but “act on impulse without thinking through consequences” sounds more like doing risky things or making snap decisions that cause problems, and I don’t really recognize myself in that as much.\n\nI do sometimes respond too quickly to something small, like clicking into something before I’ve finished what I was doing, but if you mean big impulsive actions—spending a lot of money, saying things without any filter, making major decisions suddenly—I don’t think that’s typical for me. I usually overthink those kinds of things, if anything. So… no, not often, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to relax or focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get that, especially when I’m supposed to be sitting still and paying attention for a long time.\n\nThe clearest example is long design calls at work. I’ll be on the call and I’m trying to look engaged, or at least look like I’m calmly following along, but after a while there’s this sort of pressure that builds up in my body. Not exactly anxiety, more like I just need to move. I’ll start shifting around in the chair, then maybe switch to another chair, or I’ll stand up and pace around my office while still listening.\n\nSometimes I’m aware I’m doing it and I’m trying to keep it subtle, especially if the camera is on. But if I force myself to just sit there, it actually feels harder to focus, like part of my attention is going toward not moving. So yes, I’d say I do feel restless or fidgety in those situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I think it depends what you mean by “completing.”\n\nIf it’s something where there’s a very clear endpoint and some external pressure, like someone is waiting on it, then I can usually get it done, although sometimes it takes more effort than I think it should. But if it’s a task that has a lot of parts, or the finish line is a little vague, or it’s something I started because it seemed important at the time but then there isn’t an obvious next step… that’s where I can kind of stall out.\n\nIt’s not usually that I consciously decide, “I’m not going to finish this.” It’s more like I’ll get most of the way into something, then hit a boring or messy part, or I’ll need to decide between a few options, and somehow it just stops moving. And then it sits there. Sometimes I still think about it, which is annoying, because mentally it’s not gone. It’s more like it’s hanging open in the background.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do have trouble completing tasks after I’ve started them, especially if they’re not tightly defined or if nobody is directly expecting them by a specific time. But I wouldn’t say I never finish anything. It’s more inconsistent than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or criticize yourself for no specific reason or for things that aren't entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s for no specific reason, exactly. I mean, I do criticize myself, probably more than is useful, but usually I can point to something that set it off — like I missed something, or I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have, or I feel like I made something harder than it needed to be.\n\nThe “not entirely my fault” part is harder. Sometimes I can intellectually see that there were circumstances, or that another person was involved, or that the situation was messy to begin with. But I’ll still sort of focus on the part I could have done differently. Like, “If I had just been more on top of it,” or “If I had anticipated that,” or “Why didn’t I catch that earlier?” So it’s not random guilt, but I can get pretty self-critical in a way that maybe isn’t proportional.\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of nowhere, with no trigger at all, I don’t think that’s something I really notice. It’s more tied to specific things, even if I may be taking too much responsibility for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worthless or that you're not good enough?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “worthless” is really the word I’d use. That feels stronger than what I usually experience.\n\n“Not good enough,” though… I mean, yes, that thought comes up sometimes, but I’m not sure whether I’d call it a deep belief or more like a reaction when I feel like I’ve fallen short. Like if I’m comparing what I actually got done to what I think I should have been able to do, then I can get pretty harsh with myself. I’ll think, “Why is this so hard for me?” or “Other people seem to handle this without making it into such a production.”\n\nBut it’s not like I walk around all day thinking I’m worthless as a person. It’s more situational. I can usually recognize, at least logically, that I’m competent in some ways and that I’m not a failure overall. But in the moment, when I’ve disappointed myself or I feel behind, the “not good enough” feeling can be pretty loud.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is: not worthless, generally no. Not good enough, sometimes, especially when I’m judging myself against what I think I should be able to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus is more related to having trouble paying attention or to feeling overwhelmed by stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the attention and organization piece, honestly, though stress definitely makes it worse.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t keep the moving parts organized anymore. So yes, I’d say I do feel restless or fidgety in those situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I think it depends what you mean by “completing.”\n\nIf it’s something where there’s a very clear endpoint and some external pressure, like someone is waiting on it, then I can usually get it done, although sometimes it takes more effort than I think it should. But if it’s a task that has a lot of parts, or the finish line is a little vague, or it’s something I started because it seemed important at the time but then there isn’t an obvious next step… that’s where I can kind of stall out.\n\nIt’s not usually that I consciously decide, “I’m not going to finish this.” It’s more like I’ll get most of the way into something, then hit a boring or messy part, or I’ll need to decide between a few options, and somehow it just stops moving. And then it sits there. Sometimes I still think about it, which is annoying, because mentally it’s not gone. It’s more like it’s hanging open in the background.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do have trouble completing tasks after I’ve started them, especially if they’re not tightly defined or if nobody is directly expecting them by a specific time. But I wouldn’t say I never finish anything. It’s more inconsistent than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or criticize yourself for no specific reason or for things that aren't entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s for no specific reason, exactly. I mean, I do criticize myself, probably more than is useful, but usually I can point to something that set it off — like I missed something, or I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have, or I feel like I made something harder than it needed to be.\n\nThe “not entirely my fault” part is harder. Sometimes I can intellectually see that there were circumstances, or that another person was involved, or that the situation was messy to begin with. But I’ll still sort of focus on the part I could have done differently. Like, “If I had just been more on top of it,” or “If I had anticipated that,” or “Why didn’t I catch that earlier?” So it’s not random guilt, but I can get pretty self-critical in a way that maybe isn’t proportional.\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of nowhere, with no trigger at all, I don’t think that’s something I really notice. It’s more tied to specific things, even if I may be taking too much responsibility for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worthless or that you're not good enough?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “worthless” is really the word I’d use. That feels stronger than what I usually experience.\n\n“Not good enough,” though… I mean, yes, that thought comes up sometimes, but I’m not sure whether I’d call it a deep belief or more like a reaction when I feel like I’ve fallen short. Like if I’m comparing what I actually got done to what I think I should have been able to do, then I can get pretty harsh with myself. I’ll think, “Why is this so hard for me?” or “Other people seem to handle this without making it into such a production.”\n\nBut it’s not like I walk around all day thinking I’m worthless as a person. It’s more situational. I can usually recognize, at least logically, that I’m competent in some ways and that I’m not a failure overall. But in the moment, when I’ve disappointed myself or I feel behind, the “not good enough” feeling can be pretty loud.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is: not worthless, generally no. Not good enough, sometimes, especially when I’m judging myself against what I think I should be able to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus is more related to having trouble paying attention or to feeling overwhelmed by stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the attention and organization piece, honestly, though stress definitely makes it worse.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t keep the moving parts organized anymore. It’s not just one messy desk or one late thing. It’s like I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep partly started somewhere else, gallery deadlines that I need to remember, team decisions waiting for me — and I know these things all matter, but I can’t seem to line them up in a sensible order and work through them.\n\nSo what happens is I end up starting whatever feels loudest in that moment. Like whatever email, deadline, or question is most in my face gets my attention, even if it’s not actually the most important thing. And then something else gets loud, and I switch to that. It can look from the outside like I’m busy, and I am busy, but I’m not always moving the important things forward in the right order.\n\nStress adds pressure to it, for sure. But I don’t think it starts only when I’m anxious. It feels more like I have trouble holding the whole map in my head and choosing the next right thing unless there’s some obvious external structure forcing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is more because you have trouble sustaining attention or because you're preoccupied with worries or concerns?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being preoccupied with worries, most of the time.\n\nIt’s not that I’m sitting there ruminating about something and that pulls me away. It’s more immediate and kind of embarrassingly easy to derail. Like I’ll be coding, and I’ll have the logic of something in my head — not just the code, but the whole shape of what I’m trying to solve — and then Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or I notice some visual idea or design possibility, and suddenly I’m not where I was anymore.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it doesn’t always feel like I made a decision to switch. It’s more like my attention just… grabs onto the new thing. I’ll think, “Oh, I should just check that quickly,” or “Wait, that layout idea might matter,” and then twenty minutes later I’m down some unrelated design rabbit hole and I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was originally coding.\n\nWorries can definitely be in the background sometimes, but that’s not usually the main mechanism. It’s more that my focus is fragile. If something interrupts or catches my eye, the original task sort of dissolves unless I work really hard to hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're distracted by other thoughts or stimuli?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more being distracted by other thoughts or stimuli than just boredom, though boredom probably makes it easier for that to happen.\n\nThe clearest example is when I’m coding. I can be genuinely interested in the problem, not bored at all, and still lose the thread really fast. Like if Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some visual idea pops into my head — maybe I notice a layout issue or suddenly think of a design direction — my attention just hooks onto that. And then I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just check this quickly,” but it doesn’t stay quick.\n\nWhat’s frustrating is that I can have the whole logic of the thing in my head, and then the interruption happens, and it’s like the mental structure collapses. Twenty minutes later I’ll realize I’m in some unrelated design rabbit hole, and I’m not even sure how I got there exactly. Then I come back to the code and have to reconstruct what I was thinking, which is exhausting and makes me annoyed with myself.\n\nSo, yes — boredom can make me drift, but a lot of the time it’s not boredom. It’s more that my attention is too easily pulled by whatever shows up in front of me or pops into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus would improve if you were in a quieter, less stimulating environment?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would help some. If the environment is quieter and there’s less going on, there are fewer things to grab my attention, so I’m less likely to get knocked off track.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely solve it, because it isn’t only noise in the room. It can be really small things. Like if I’m coding and I’ve got the logic of the problem in my head, a Slack ping can pull me out of it. Or a browser tab flashes, or I notice something visual — even just an idea about a layout or design direction — and suddenly I’m following that instead.\n\nAnd it feels so fast. I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just check that for a second,” and then somehow twenty minutes are gone and I’m in some unrelated design rabbit hole. Then when I come back to the code, I’ve lost the thread and I have to rebuild the whole mental picture again.\n\nSo, yes, quieter and less stimulating would definitely reduce the number of triggers. But even in a quiet setting, if there’s something on the screen or something pops into my head, I can still get pulled away pretty easily.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "Determine if the patient's symptoms are primarily related to attention deficit or anxiety", "question": "Do you think your difficulty with focus and organization is something you've experienced throughout your life, or is it a more recent development?"}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely not just recent. Looking back, it was there when I was a kid too, even in grade school.

I didn’t really understand it that way at the time, obviously. I think it got framed more like I was careless or not trying consistently, because I was considered bright and I could do the work. But my teachers would write things like I was bright but daydreamed, or that I wasn’t turning things in. And the especially ridiculous part is that sometimes the assignments were actually finished — I just forgot to hand them in or they disappeared into my desk or backpack somehow.

I also lost homework folders a lot. Like, long before I was a teenager, that was already a pattern. Folders, papers, things I needed for class — I’d misplace them and then be scrambling, even though I wasn’t trying to avoid the work. So, when I look back now, it feels like the same basic issue has been there for a long time. It’s just changed shape as the demands got bigger.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F43.0, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Yeah, um… I guess the short version is that I’m here because I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts of my life organized anymore. It’s gotten to the point where I’m sort of constantly juggling things—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and instead of being able to step back and decide what actually matters most, I end up just reacting to whatever is loudest or most urgent-looking in the moment.\n\nAnd I know everyone gets busy, but this feels different. It’s like the pieces are all there, but I can’t get them into a usable order. I’ll start one thing because it’s making the most noise, then realize something more important was due, or that I’ve neglected a decision someone was waiting on. That’s really what finally pushed me to come in. I’m spending a lot of energy, but it doesn’t feel like I’m steering it very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing on a single task for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, and it’s not always that I’m bored with the task, which is what’s frustrating. Like, I can be in the middle of coding something I actually care about, and I’ll have the whole structure in my head—what I’m trying to change, which file I’m in, why I opened it—and then Slack pings, or some little notification flashes in a browser tab, or even just a visual idea pops into my head, like “oh, that layout could work better if…” and suddenly I’ve lost the original thread.\n\nAnd then I’m not just distracted for thirty seconds. I’ll follow that thing. I’ll click over, check the message, maybe open another tab, look at some unrelated design reference, tweak something that wasn’t the point, and twenty minutes later I’m sitting there thinking, “Wait, what was I actually supposed to be doing?” It feels like my attention gets pulled sideways really easily, especially if the interruption has any novelty or visual hook to it. Then getting back into the original task takes more effort than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things even when they are going well?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d describe it as worrying when things are going well, exactly. I mean, I can second-guess whether I’m missing something, but I don’t know if that’s the same as worry in the way you mean it.\n\nLike if something is actually going smoothly, I don’t sit there feeling afraid something bad is definitely going to happen. It’s more that I might have this background sense of, “Okay, what am I forgetting?” But honestly I’m not sure whether that’s worry or just… trying to keep track of things. So I don’t know that I’ve noticed that as a major thing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious more often than not, even when there's no obvious reason to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least, not in the sense of feeling anxious most of the time for no clear reason.\n\nI can get tense when there’s something specific I’m trying to keep track of, or if I have that feeling that I may have forgotten something, but that feels tied to actual stuff going on rather than anxiety just showing up out of nowhere. If I’m sitting quietly and nothing obvious is pressing, I’m not usually in a constant anxious state.\n\nI guess I’d say I can be uneasy when I’m trying to mentally hold too many things at once, but I wouldn’t describe myself as anxious more often than not.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can definitely switch to something quickly if it catches my attention, but “act on impulse without thinking through consequences” sounds more like doing risky things or making snap decisions that cause problems, and I don’t really recognize myself in that as much.\n\nI do sometimes respond too quickly to something small, like clicking into something before I’ve finished what I was doing, but if you mean big impulsive actions—spending a lot of money, saying things without any filter, making major decisions suddenly—I don’t think that’s typical for me. I usually overthink those kinds of things, if anything. So… no, not often, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to relax or focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get that, especially when I’m supposed to be sitting still and paying attention for a long time.\n\nThe clearest example is long design calls at work. I’ll be on the call and I’m trying to look engaged, or at least look like I’m calmly following along, but after a while there’s this sort of pressure that builds up in my body. Not exactly anxiety, more like I just need to move. I’ll start shifting around in the chair, then maybe switch to another chair, or I’ll stand up and pace around my office while still listening.\n\nSometimes I’m aware I’m doing it and I’m trying to keep it subtle, especially if the camera is on. But if I force myself to just sit there, it actually feels harder to focus, like part of my attention is going toward not moving. So yes, I’d say I do feel restless or fidgety in those situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I think it depends what you mean by “completing.”\n\nIf it’s something where there’s a very clear endpoint and some external pressure, like someone is waiting on it, then I can usually get it done, although sometimes it takes more effort than I think it should. But if it’s a task that has a lot of parts, or the finish line is a little vague, or it’s something I started because it seemed important at the time but then there isn’t an obvious next step… that’s where I can kind of stall out.\n\nIt’s not usually that I consciously decide, “I’m not going to finish this.” It’s more like I’ll get most of the way into something, then hit a boring or messy part, or I’ll need to decide between a few options, and somehow it just stops moving. And then it sits there. Sometimes I still think about it, which is annoying, because mentally it’s not gone. It’s more like it’s hanging open in the background.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do have trouble completing tasks after I’ve started them, especially if they’re not tightly defined or if nobody is directly expecting them by a specific time. But I wouldn’t say I never finish anything. It’s more inconsistent than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or criticize yourself for no specific reason or for things that aren't entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s for no specific reason, exactly. I mean, I do criticize myself, probably more than is useful, but usually I can point to something that set it off — like I missed something, or I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have, or I feel like I made something harder than it needed to be.\n\nThe “not entirely my fault” part is harder. Sometimes I can intellectually see that there were circumstances, or that another person was involved, or that the situation was messy to begin with. But I’ll still sort of focus on the part I could have done differently. Like, “If I had just been more on top of it,” or “If I had anticipated that,” or “Why didn’t I catch that earlier?” So it’s not random guilt, but I can get pretty self-critical in a way that maybe isn’t proportional.\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of nowhere, with no trigger at all, I don’t think that’s something I really notice. It’s more tied to specific things, even if I may be taking too much responsibility for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worthless or that you're not good enough?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “worthless” is really the word I’d use. That feels stronger than what I usually experience.\n\n“Not good enough,” though… I mean, yes, that thought comes up sometimes, but I’m not sure whether I’d call it a deep belief or more like a reaction when I feel like I’ve fallen short. Like if I’m comparing what I actually got done to what I think I should have been able to do, then I can get pretty harsh with myself. I’ll think, “Why is this so hard for me?” or “Other people seem to handle this without making it into such a production.”\n\nBut it’s not like I walk around all day thinking I’m worthless as a person. It’s more situational. I can usually recognize, at least logically, that I’m competent in some ways and that I’m not a failure overall. But in the moment, when I’ve disappointed myself or I feel behind, the “not good enough” feeling can be pretty loud.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is: not worthless, generally no. Not good enough, sometimes, especially when I’m judging myself against what I think I should be able to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus is more related to having trouble paying attention or to feeling overwhelmed by stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the attention and organization piece, honestly, though stress definitely makes it worse.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t keep the moving parts organized anymore. It’s not just one messy desk or one late thing. It’s like I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep partly started somewhere else, gallery deadlines that I need to remember, team decisions waiting for me — and I know these things all matter, but I can’t seem to line them up in a sensible order and work through them.\n\nSo what happens is I end up starting whatever feels loudest in that moment. Like whatever email, deadline, or question is most in my face gets my attention, even if it’s not actually the most important thing. And then something else gets loud, and I switch to that. It can look from the outside like I’m busy, and I am busy, but I’m not always moving the important things forward in the right order.\n\nStress adds pressure to it, for sure. But I don’t think it starts only when I’m anxious. It feels more like I have trouble holding the whole map in my head and choosing the next right thing unless there’s some obvious external structure forcing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is more because you have trouble sustaining attention or because you're preoccupied with worries or concerns?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being preoccupied with worries, most of the time.\n\nIt’s not that I’m sitting there ruminating about something and that pulls me away. It’s more immediate and kind of embarrassingly easy to derail. Like I’ll be coding, and I’ll have the logic of something in my head — not just the code, but the whole shape of what I’m trying to solve — and then Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or I notice some visual idea or design possibility, and suddenly I’m not where I was anymore.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it doesn’t always feel like I made a decision to switch. It’s more like my attention just… grabs onto the new thing. I’ll think, “Oh, I should just check that quickly,” or “Wait, that layout idea might matter,” and then twenty minutes later I’m down some unrelated design rabbit hole and I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was originally coding.\n\nWorries can definitely be in the background sometimes, but that’s not usually the main mechanism. It’s more that my focus is fragile. If something interrupts or catches my eye, the original task sort of dissolves unless I work really hard to hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're distracted by other thoughts or stimuli?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more being distracted by other thoughts or stimuli than just boredom, though boredom probably makes it easier for that to happen.\n\nThe clearest example is when I’m coding. I can be genuinely interested in the problem, not bored at all, and still lose the thread really fast. Like if Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some visual idea pops into my head — maybe I notice a layout issue or suddenly think of a design direction — my attention just hooks onto that. And then I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just check this quickly,” but it doesn’t stay quick.\n\nWhat’s frustrating is that I can have the whole logic of the thing in my head, and then the interruption happens, and it’s like the mental structure collapses. Twenty minutes later I’ll realize I’m in some unrelated design rabbit hole, and I’m not even sure how I got there exactly. Then I come back to the code and have to reconstruct what I was thinking, which is exhausting and makes me annoyed with myself.\n\nSo, yes — boredom can make me drift, but a lot of the time it’s not boredom. It’s more that my attention is too easily pulled by whatever shows up in front of me or pops into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus would improve if you were in a quieter, less stimulating environment?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would help some. If the environment is quieter and there’s less going on, there are fewer things to grab my attention, so I’m less likely to get knocked off track.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely solve it, because it isn’t only noise in the room. It can be really small things. Like if I’m coding and I’ve got the logic of the problem in my head, a Slack ping can pull me out of it. Or a browser tab flashes, or I notice something visual — even just an idea about a layout or design direction — and suddenly I’m following that instead.\n\nAnd it feels so fast. I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just check that for a second,” and then somehow twenty minutes are gone and I’m in some unrelated design rabbit hole. Then when I come back to the code, I’ve lost the thread and I have to rebuild the whole mental picture again.\n\nSo, yes, quieter and less stimulating would definitely reduce the number of triggers. But even in a quiet setting, if there’s something on the screen or something pops into my head, I can still get pulled away pretty easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and organization is something you've experienced throughout your life, or is it a more recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just recent. Looking back, it was there when I was a kid too, even in grade school.\n\nI didn’t really understand it that way at the time, obviously. I think it got framed more like I was careless or not trying consistently, because I was considered bright and I could do the work. But my teachers would write things like I was bright but daydreamed, or that I wasn’t turning things in. And the especially ridiculous part is that sometimes the assignments were actually finished — I just forgot to hand them in or they disappeared into my desk or backpack somehow.\n\nI also lost homework folders a lot. Like, long before I was a teenager, that was already a pattern. Folders, papers, things I needed for class — I’d misplace them and then be scrambling, even though I wasn’t trying to avoid the work. So, when I look back now, it feels like the same basic issue has been there for a long time. It’s just changed shape as the demands got bigger.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yes. I mean, unfortunately, yes — especially in daily life at work. I think for a long time I could kind of compensate or smooth things over, and people still saw me as capable. But lately it feels like it’s starting to affect my reputation in a way I can’t just explain away.

One example that really bothered me was I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing. It wasn’t that I didn’t care about it — I did — but I didn’t stay organized around the timing and follow-through, and by the time I realized it, it had already become a problem. That kind of thing makes me look unreliable, even when the actual technical work isn’t the issue.

And then there was a difficult architecture review where I just didn’t handle it as cleanly as I needed to. There were a lot of moving parts and collaborators depending on me to lead the discussion and keep the structure clear. Afterward, I got the sense that some people were questioning whether I could really lead the larger project. That was hard to take, because I know I understand the system and I care about the work, but the organization and follow-through piece made me look less capable than I think I actually am.

So yes, it’s not just an internal frustration anymore. It’s affecting how people see me professionally, and that has started to feel pretty serious.

Very much, and it’s not always that I’m bored with the task, which is what’s frustrating. Like, I can be in the middle of coding something I actually care about, and I’ll have the whole structure in my head—what I’m trying to change, which file I’m in, why I opened it—and then Slack pings, or some little notification flashes in a browser tab, or even just a visual idea pops into my head, like “oh, that layout could work better if…” and suddenly I’ve lost the original thread.\n\nAnd then I’m not just distracted for thirty seconds. It’s more that I might have this background sense of, “Okay, what am I forgetting?” But honestly I’m not sure whether that’s worry or just… trying to keep track of things. So I don’t know that I’ve noticed that as a major thing, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious more often than not, even when there's no obvious reason to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least, not in the sense of feeling anxious most of the time for no clear reason.\n\nI can get tense when there’s something specific I’m trying to keep track of, or if I have that feeling that I may have forgotten something, but that feels tied to actual stuff going on rather than anxiety just showing up out of nowhere. If I’m sitting quietly and nothing obvious is pressing, I’m not usually in a constant anxious state.\n\nI guess I’d say I can be uneasy when I’m trying to mentally hold too many things at once, but I wouldn’t describe myself as anxious more often than not.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a big thing for me. I mean, I can definitely switch to something quickly if it catches my attention, but “act on impulse without thinking through consequences” sounds more like doing risky things or making snap decisions that cause problems, and I don’t really recognize myself in that as much.\n\nI do sometimes respond too quickly to something small, like clicking into something before I’ve finished what I was doing, but if you mean big impulsive actions—spending a lot of money, saying things without any filter, making major decisions suddenly—I don’t think that’s typical for me. I usually overthink those kinds of things, if anything. So… no, not often, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're trying to relax or focus?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get that, especially when I’m supposed to be sitting still and paying attention for a long time.\n\nThe clearest example is long design calls at work. I’ll be on the call and I’m trying to look engaged, or at least look like I’m calmly following along, but after a while there’s this sort of pressure that builds up in my body. Not exactly anxiety, more like I just need to move. I’ll start shifting around in the chair, then maybe switch to another chair, or I’ll stand up and pace around my office while still listening.\n\nSometimes I’m aware I’m doing it and I’m trying to keep it subtle, especially if the camera is on. But if I force myself to just sit there, it actually feels harder to focus, like part of my attention is going toward not moving. So yes, I’d say I do feel restless or fidgety in those situations.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks once you've started them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I think it depends what you mean by “completing.”\n\nIf it’s something where there’s a very clear endpoint and some external pressure, like someone is waiting on it, then I can usually get it done, although sometimes it takes more effort than I think it should. But if it’s a task that has a lot of parts, or the finish line is a little vague, or it’s something I started because it seemed important at the time but then there isn’t an obvious next step… that’s where I can kind of stall out.\n\nIt’s not usually that I consciously decide, “I’m not going to finish this.” It’s more like I’ll get most of the way into something, then hit a boring or messy part, or I’ll need to decide between a few options, and somehow it just stops moving. And then it sits there. Sometimes I still think about it, which is annoying, because mentally it’s not gone. It’s more like it’s hanging open in the background.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do have trouble completing tasks after I’ve started them, especially if they’re not tightly defined or if nobody is directly expecting them by a specific time. But I wouldn’t say I never finish anything. It’s more inconsistent than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or criticize yourself for no specific reason or for things that aren't entirely your fault?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s for no specific reason, exactly. I mean, I do criticize myself, probably more than is useful, but usually I can point to something that set it off — like I missed something, or I didn’t handle something the way I think I should have, or I feel like I made something harder than it needed to be.\n\nThe “not entirely my fault” part is harder. Sometimes I can intellectually see that there were circumstances, or that another person was involved, or that the situation was messy to begin with. But I’ll still sort of focus on the part I could have done differently. Like, “If I had just been more on top of it,” or “If I had anticipated that,” or “Why didn’t I catch that earlier?” So it’s not random guilt, but I can get pretty self-critical in a way that maybe isn’t proportional.\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of nowhere, with no trigger at all, I don’t think that’s something I really notice. It’s more tied to specific things, even if I may be taking too much responsibility for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worthless or that you're not good enough?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “worthless” is really the word I’d use. That feels stronger than what I usually experience.\n\n“Not good enough,” though… I mean, yes, that thought comes up sometimes, but I’m not sure whether I’d call it a deep belief or more like a reaction when I feel like I’ve fallen short. Like if I’m comparing what I actually got done to what I think I should have been able to do, then I can get pretty harsh with myself. I’ll think, “Why is this so hard for me?” or “Other people seem to handle this without making it into such a production.”\n\nBut it’s not like I walk around all day thinking I’m worthless as a person. It’s more situational. I can usually recognize, at least logically, that I’m competent in some ways and that I’m not a failure overall. But in the moment, when I’ve disappointed myself or I feel behind, the “not good enough” feeling can be pretty loud.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is: not worthless, generally no. Not good enough, sometimes, especially when I’m judging myself against what I think I should be able to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus is more related to having trouble paying attention or to feeling overwhelmed by stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the attention and organization piece, honestly, though stress definitely makes it worse.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t keep the moving parts organized anymore. It’s not just one messy desk or one late thing. It’s like I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep partly started somewhere else, gallery deadlines that I need to remember, team decisions waiting for me — and I know these things all matter, but I can’t seem to line them up in a sensible order and work through them.\n\nSo what happens is I end up starting whatever feels loudest in that moment. Like whatever email, deadline, or question is most in my face gets my attention, even if it’s not actually the most important thing. And then something else gets loud, and I switch to that. It can look from the outside like I’m busy, and I am busy, but I’m not always moving the important things forward in the right order.\n\nStress adds pressure to it, for sure. But I don’t think it starts only when I’m anxious. It feels more like I have trouble holding the whole map in my head and choosing the next right thing unless there’s some obvious external structure forcing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is more because you have trouble sustaining attention or because you're preoccupied with worries or concerns?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being preoccupied with worries, most of the time.\n\nIt’s not that I’m sitting there ruminating about something and that pulls me away. It’s more immediate and kind of embarrassingly easy to derail. Like I’ll be coding, and I’ll have the logic of something in my head — not just the code, but the whole shape of what I’m trying to solve — and then Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or I notice some visual idea or design possibility, and suddenly I’m not where I was anymore.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it doesn’t always feel like I made a decision to switch. It’s more like my attention just… grabs onto the new thing. I’ll think, “Oh, I should just check that quickly,” or “Wait, that layout idea might matter,” and then twenty minutes later I’m down some unrelated design rabbit hole and I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was originally coding.\n\nWorries can definitely be in the background sometimes, but that’s not usually the main mechanism. It’s more that my focus is fragile. If something interrupts or catches my eye, the original task sort of dissolves unless I work really hard to hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're distracted by other thoughts or stimuli?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more being distracted by other thoughts or stimuli than just boredom, though boredom probably makes it easier for that to happen.\n\nThe clearest example is when I’m coding. I can be genuinely interested in the problem, not bored at all, and still lose the thread really fast. Like if Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some visual idea pops into my head — maybe I notice a layout issue or suddenly think of a design direction — my attention just hooks onto that. And then I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just check this quickly,” but it doesn’t stay quick.\n\nWhat’s frustrating is that I can have the whole logic of the thing in my head, and then the interruption happens, and it’s like the mental structure collapses. Twenty minutes later I’ll realize I’m in some unrelated design rabbit hole, and I’m not even sure how I got there exactly. Then I come back to the code and have to reconstruct what I was thinking, which is exhausting and makes me annoyed with myself.\n\nSo, yes — boredom can make me drift, but a lot of the time it’s not boredom. It’s more that my attention is too easily pulled by whatever shows up in front of me or pops into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus would improve if you were in a quieter, less stimulating environment?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would help some. If the environment is quieter and there’s less going on, there are fewer things to grab my attention, so I’m less likely to get knocked off track.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely solve it, because it isn’t only noise in the room. It can be really small things. Like if I’m coding and I’ve got the logic of the problem in my head, a Slack ping can pull me out of it. Or a browser tab flashes, or I notice something visual — even just an idea about a layout or design direction — and suddenly I’m following that instead.\n\nAnd it feels so fast. I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just check that for a second,” and then somehow twenty minutes are gone and I’m in some unrelated design rabbit hole. Then when I come back to the code, I’ve lost the thread and I have to rebuild the whole mental picture again.\n\nSo, yes, quieter and less stimulating would definitely reduce the number of triggers. But even in a quiet setting, if there’s something on the screen or something pops into my head, I can still get pulled away pretty easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and organization is something you've experienced throughout your life, or is it a more recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just recent. Looking back, it was there when I was a kid too, even in grade school.\n\nI didn’t really understand it that way at the time, obviously. I think it got framed more like I was careless or not trying consistently, because I was considered bright and I could do the work. But my teachers would write things like I was bright but daydreamed, or that I wasn’t turning things in. And the especially ridiculous part is that sometimes the assignments were actually finished — I just forgot to hand them in or they disappeared into my desk or backpack somehow.\n\nI also lost homework folders a lot. Like, long before I was a teenager, that was already a pattern. Folders, papers, things I needed for class — I’d misplace them and then be scrambling, even though I wasn’t trying to avoid the work. So, when I look back now, it feels like the same basic issue has been there for a long time. It’s just changed shape as the demands got bigger.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus significantly affected your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, unfortunately, yes — especially in daily life at work. I think for a long time I could kind of compensate or smooth things over, and people still saw me as capable. But lately it feels like it’s starting to affect my reputation in a way I can’t just explain away.\n\nOne example that really bothered me was I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing. It wasn’t that I didn’t care about it — I did — but I didn’t stay organized around the timing and follow-through, and by the time I realized it, it had already become a problem. That kind of thing makes me look unreliable, even when the actual technical work isn’t the issue.\n\nAnd then there was a difficult architecture review where I just didn’t handle it as cleanly as I needed to. There were a lot of moving parts and collaborators depending on me to lead the discussion and keep the structure clear. Afterward, I got the sense that some people were questioning whether I could really lead the larger project. That kind of thing makes me look unreliable, even when the actual technical work isn’t the issue.\n\nAnd then there was a difficult architecture review where I just didn’t handle it as cleanly as I needed to. There were a lot of moving parts and collaborators depending on me to lead the discussion and keep the structure clear.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more attention than anxiety, honestly. I mean, stress can make it worse, sure, but the thing that happens feels very mechanical and immediate, not like I’m sitting there worrying and then can’t focus.

Like, if I’m coding and I’ve got the structure of the problem in my head — all the variables and edge cases and the thing I’m trying to hold together — then one Slack ping can just knock me out of it. Or a browser tab flashes, or I see some little visual thing and suddenly I’m thinking about a layout idea or a design direction, and before I’ve really made a decision to switch tasks, I’m already gone.

And the frustrating part is that it doesn’t feel emotionally driven most of the time. It’s more like my attention gets grabbed. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just look at this for ten seconds,” and then it turns into twenty minutes down some unrelated design rabbit hole. Then when I come back to the code, I’ve lost the whole thread and I have to reconstruct it from scratch.

So, yes, anxiety or pressure probably adds to it sometimes, especially once I realize I’m behind. But the original problem feels much more like I’m distractible and have trouble staying anchored to the thing I meant to be doing.

Then I come back to the code and have to reconstruct what I was thinking, which is exhausting and makes me annoyed with myself.\n\nSo, yes — boredom can make me drift, but a lot of the time it’s not boredom. It’s more that my attention is too easily pulled by whatever shows up in front of me or pops into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus would improve if you were in a quieter, less stimulating environment?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would help some. If the environment is quieter and there’s less going on, there are fewer things to grab my attention, so I’m less likely to get knocked off track.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely solve it, because it isn’t only noise in the room. Sometimes I can intellectually see that there were circumstances, or that another person was involved, or that the situation was messy to begin with. But I’ll still sort of focus on the part I could have done differently. Like, “If I had just been more on top of it,” or “If I had anticipated that,” or “Why didn’t I catch that earlier?” So it’s not random guilt, but I can get pretty self-critical in a way that maybe isn’t proportional.\n\nBut if you mean feeling guilty out of nowhere, with no trigger at all, I don’t think that’s something I really notice. It’s more tied to specific things, even if I may be taking too much responsibility for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're worthless or that you're not good enough?\n\nPatient: I don’t think “worthless” is really the word I’d use. That feels stronger than what I usually experience.\n\n“Not good enough,” though… I mean, yes, that thought comes up sometimes, but I’m not sure whether I’d call it a deep belief or more like a reaction when I feel like I’ve fallen short. Like if I’m comparing what I actually got done to what I think I should have been able to do, then I can get pretty harsh with myself. I’ll think, “Why is this so hard for me?” or “Other people seem to handle this without making it into such a production.”\n\nBut it’s not like I walk around all day thinking I’m worthless as a person. It’s more situational. I can usually recognize, at least logically, that I’m competent in some ways and that I’m not a failure overall. But in the moment, when I’ve disappointed myself or I feel behind, the “not good enough” feeling can be pretty loud.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is: not worthless, generally no. Not good enough, sometimes, especially when I’m judging myself against what I think I should be able to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus is more related to having trouble paying attention or to feeling overwhelmed by stress and anxiety?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the attention and organization piece, honestly, though stress definitely makes it worse.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I feel like I can’t keep the moving parts organized anymore. It’s not just one messy desk or one late thing. It’s like I’ll have patent notes in one place, workshop prep partly started somewhere else, gallery deadlines that I need to remember, team decisions waiting for me — and I know these things all matter, but I can’t seem to line them up in a sensible order and work through them.\n\nSo what happens is I end up starting whatever feels loudest in that moment. Like whatever email, deadline, or question is most in my face gets my attention, even if it’s not actually the most important thing. And then something else gets loud, and I switch to that. It can look from the outside like I’m busy, and I am busy, but I’m not always moving the important things forward in the right order.\n\nStress adds pressure to it, for sure. But I don’t think it starts only when I’m anxious. It feels more like I have trouble holding the whole map in my head and choosing the next right thing unless there’s some obvious external structure forcing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with staying focused is more because you have trouble sustaining attention or because you're preoccupied with worries or concerns?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more trouble sustaining attention than being preoccupied with worries, most of the time.\n\nIt’s not that I’m sitting there ruminating about something and that pulls me away. It’s more immediate and kind of embarrassingly easy to derail. Like I’ll be coding, and I’ll have the logic of something in my head — not just the code, but the whole shape of what I’m trying to solve — and then Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or I notice some visual idea or design possibility, and suddenly I’m not where I was anymore.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it doesn’t always feel like I made a decision to switch. It’s more like my attention just… grabs onto the new thing. I’ll think, “Oh, I should just check that quickly,” or “Wait, that layout idea might matter,” and then twenty minutes later I’m down some unrelated design rabbit hole and I’ve completely lost the thread of what I was originally coding.\n\nWorries can definitely be in the background sometimes, but that’s not usually the main mechanism. It’s more that my focus is fragile. If something interrupts or catches my eye, the original task sort of dissolves unless I work really hard to hold onto it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mind wanders because you're bored with the task or because you're distracted by other thoughts or stimuli?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more being distracted by other thoughts or stimuli than just boredom, though boredom probably makes it easier for that to happen.\n\nThe clearest example is when I’m coding. I can be genuinely interested in the problem, not bored at all, and still lose the thread really fast. Like if Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some visual idea pops into my head — maybe I notice a layout issue or suddenly think of a design direction — my attention just hooks onto that. And then I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just check this quickly,” but it doesn’t stay quick.\n\nWhat’s frustrating is that I can have the whole logic of the thing in my head, and then the interruption happens, and it’s like the mental structure collapses. Twenty minutes later I’ll realize I’m in some unrelated design rabbit hole, and I’m not even sure how I got there exactly. Then I come back to the code and have to reconstruct what I was thinking, which is exhausting and makes me annoyed with myself.\n\nSo, yes — boredom can make me drift, but a lot of the time it’s not boredom. It’s more that my attention is too easily pulled by whatever shows up in front of me or pops into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with organization and focus would improve if you were in a quieter, less stimulating environment?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would help some. If the environment is quieter and there’s less going on, there are fewer things to grab my attention, so I’m less likely to get knocked off track.\n\nBut I don’t think it would completely solve it, because it isn’t only noise in the room. It can be really small things. Like if I’m coding and I’ve got the logic of the problem in my head, a Slack ping can pull me out of it. Or a browser tab flashes, or I notice something visual — even just an idea about a layout or design direction — and suddenly I’m following that instead.\n\nAnd it feels so fast. I’ll think, “Okay, I’ll just check that for a second,” and then somehow twenty minutes are gone and I’m in some unrelated design rabbit hole. Then when I come back to the code, I’ve lost the thread and I have to rebuild the whole mental picture again.\n\nSo, yes, quieter and less stimulating would definitely reduce the number of triggers. But even in a quiet setting, if there’s something on the screen or something pops into my head, I can still get pulled away pretty easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and organization is something you've experienced throughout your life, or is it a more recent development?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely not just recent. Looking back, it was there when I was a kid too, even in grade school.\n\nI didn’t really understand it that way at the time, obviously. I think it got framed more like I was careless or not trying consistently, because I was considered bright and I could do the work. But my teachers would write things like I was bright but daydreamed, or that I wasn’t turning things in. And the especially ridiculous part is that sometimes the assignments were actually finished — I just forgot to hand them in or they disappeared into my desk or backpack somehow.\n\nI also lost homework folders a lot. Like, long before I was a teenager, that was already a pattern. Folders, papers, things I needed for class — I’d misplace them and then be scrambling, even though I wasn’t trying to avoid the work. So, when I look back now, it feels like the same basic issue has been there for a long time. It’s just changed shape as the demands got bigger.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus significantly affected your relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, unfortunately, yes — especially in daily life at work. I think for a long time I could kind of compensate or smooth things over, and people still saw me as capable. But lately it feels like it’s starting to affect my reputation in a way I can’t just explain away.\n\nOne example that really bothered me was I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing. It wasn’t that I didn’t care about it — I did — but I didn’t stay organized around the timing and follow-through, and by the time I realized it, it had already become a problem. That kind of thing makes me look unreliable, even when the actual technical work isn’t the issue.\n\nAnd then there was a difficult architecture review where I just didn’t handle it as cleanly as I needed to. There were a lot of moving parts and collaborators depending on me to lead the discussion and keep the structure clear. Afterward, I got the sense that some people were questioning whether I could really lead the larger project. That was hard to take, because I know I understand the system and I care about the work, but the organization and follow-through piece made me look less capable than I think I actually am.\n\nSo yes, it’s not just an internal frustration anymore. It’s affecting how people see me professionally, and that has started to feel pretty serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and organization are more closely related to problems with attention or to feelings of anxiety and stress?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more attention than anxiety, honestly. I mean, stress can make it worse, sure, but the thing that happens feels very mechanical and immediate, not like I’m sitting there worrying and then can’t focus.\n\nLike, if I’m coding and I’ve got the structure of the problem in my head — all the variables and edge cases and the thing I’m trying to hold together — then one Slack ping can just knock me out of it. Or a browser tab flashes, or I see some little visual thing and suddenly I’m thinking about a layout idea or a design direction, and before I’ve really made a decision to switch tasks, I’m already gone.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is that it doesn’t feel emotionally driven most of the time. It’s more like my attention gets grabbed. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll just look at this for ten seconds,” and then it turns into twenty minutes down some unrelated design rabbit hole. Then when I come back to the code, I’ve lost the whole thread and I have to reconstruct it from scratch.\n\nSo, yes, anxiety or pressure probably adds to it sometimes, especially once I realize I’m behind. But the original problem feels much more like I’m distractible and have trouble staying anchored to the thing I meant to be doing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it would help somewhat, but I don’t think it would solve the main problem. Stress definitely makes me more brittle, if that makes sense, but even when I’m not especially anxious, I can still get pulled off task really easily.

Like if I’m coding and I’ve got the whole structure in my head, and then Slack pings, or a browser tab flashes, or even some visual idea pops into my mind, I can lose the thread almost immediately. It’s not always that I’m stressed and then distracted — it’s more like my attention just latches onto the new thing before I’ve had a chance to stop it.

And then I’ll think, okay, I’ll just check this quickly, or I’ll just look at this design idea for a second, and suddenly twenty minutes have gone into some unrelated rabbit hole. By the time I get back to the original code, the mental model is gone and I have to rebuild it. So managing stress would probably reduce some of the fallout, but the distractibility itself feels like it would still be there.
